### The project will look at a series of parameters to monitor and assess forests, orchards and rangelands and calculate carbon stocks. These are:  

- Land productivity dynamic indicator for each parcel identified and georeferenced (cf. Annex 2 page 179);  
- Forest cover changes >10% via Hansen Forest Cover Change methodology and field observations (cf. Annex 2 page 179);  
- Hectares with survival rate >65% (cf. Annex 2 page 179).  

Monitoring metrics will also depend from:  

- Tree species data according to existing composition of forests in target areas (Spruce forests, Walnut forest, Juniper forest, Pistachio forest, Poplar forest and Willows and Gummy forests as shrub and riparian forests). References include: Baseline Atlas (Annex 6.b), the Feasibility Study (Annex 2, pages 22- 23; 31-32: 110-114), and the Working Paper (WP) on Forestry in Annex 9.  
- Age of trees/Growth rate: for the purposes of the monitoring carbon benefits during the project, the age of existing stands in target areas is classified according to Kraft classification as reported in the Forest Typology in the Kyrgyz Republic of 2008 as well as in the National Forest Assessment carried out by FAO and SAEPF in 2010. (Forestry Working Paper), the FAO’s Global Ecological Zones of 2011 and consultation with national and local stakeholders. Growth rates of forests in target areas are reported in Annex 2 (pages 225-226).  
- Forest cover according to Hansen forest cover of georeferenced plots.  
- Land Productivity Dynamics (LPD) of georeferenced plots.

# ANNEXES

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 96 OF 81

FAO confirms that the default parameters will be monitored during the project and reported annually.

Georeferencing of activities and geospatial analysis (Earth Map, introduced in Chapter 6 of the Feasibility Study, page 173, as well as in Chapter 1 (page 20, paragraphs 78) and subsequent of the FS, and presented in details in Annex 6.b of the submission package) will provide the project with data on investments' distribution and improvements against baseline (e.g. LPD, NDVI, Hansen Forest Cover Change). Results will be disaggregated per district, community and type of investment. Results will be presented on an annual basis and will be included in the Project Annual Report.

Georeferencing of all investments and using remote sensing combined with ground monitoring (jointly with stakeholders) will allow identifying impacts and attributions. The georeferenced archive will provide precise indication of where activities are executed (quantitative analysis) and associate to them the attributes identified by the baseline (qualitative analysis). This approach will allow the project and the AE to follow up via ground-based surveys and remote sensing analysis the evolution of the default parameters.

The two main relevant institutions to ensure long-term sustainability of carbon accounting and of other geospatial tools are the SAEPF (GCF NDA), the Pasture Department in the Ministry of Agriculture and Food Industry (MAFIM) and the Statistic Committee. Knowledge transfer to these institutions will start from the initial stage of the project until full handover at the end of the project. The SAEPF, also covering the role of NDA, is the institution tasked to report to the UNFCCC. This element represents a significant institutional incentive to embed carbon accounting within its functions. FAO has already provided stakeholders with a tailored Russian version of both E X-ACT and GLEAM-i to ensure full ownership and control of the tool and related processes. For example, SAEPF has already received exposure and training to EX-ACT and trainings. Refreshers trainings are budgeted within Activity 1.2.1 throughout the project duration. This will be coupled with on-the-job learning during project implementation. Additionally in addition to the work done during the national engagement process, identified stakeholders will be trained on GLEAM-i. MAFIM also showed interest in receiving training on GLEAM-i, and a draft Russian version of the tool has been prepared specifically for this project.

Additional opportunity for long term adoption of the relevant monitoring tools and techniques (including remote sensing) is provided by the signature in 2016 of the Kyrgyzstan National Spatial Infrastructure Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) (cf. Annex 9, WP on Georeferencing Strategy). This MoU, signed among others by SAEPF and MAFIM, aims at the standardization and sharing of national spatial data, including in the use of remote sensing for natural resources monitoring, management and planning. These signatory institutions will be trained (as part of Activity 1.2.1) to use the appropriate remote sensing tools and technologies. The momentum generated by the signature of the memorandum constitute a unique occasion to promote further georeferencing among national institutions and other development actors, such as the WB, GIZ, IFAD and FAO.

## Deforestation/illegal loggings

According to different sources, including 2017 Collect Earth assessment within the framework of the FAO implemented project "National Forest Monitoring and Information Systems for a transparent and truthfull REDD+" (https://www.international-climate-initiative.com/en/details/project/332/), and the University of Maryland and the Hansen's forest cover change dataset results (2000-2018), the deforestation rate for Kyrgyzstan ranges from 0 to 0.38% at national level and 0.3% in target areas.

Each of the areas identified for forest investments is georeferenced and accurately analysed in the baseline (Baseline Atlas, Annex 6.b). The project will constantly monitor the state of forests in target areas.

I areas via dedicated remote sensing analysis and involvement of communities in the monitoring processes as described in Annex 2 Chapter 6 as well as in the georeferencing strategy working paper (Annex 9). Furthermore, FAO will identify a control area external to project's intervention to better quantify and follow up on project's impacts. The identified procedure will allow the project and FAO to constantly monitor activities against the baseline and to provide objective and tangible data for carbon calculation and independent impact evaluations.

Given the nature and scope of the project and as part of the project's aim to support the country in creating the enabling environment for an innovative evidence-based climate and natural resource planning and monitoring system, deforestation is already part of the project monitoring activity in target areas. Furthermore, the tools developed by FAO (e.g. Collect Earth, Earth Map, EX-ACT, GLEAM-i) and practices (e.g. Georeferencing, geospatial analysis and community monitoring) have already been introduced to the SAEPF and the Pasture Departments in order to ensure full ownership of the process as well as scaling up of this approach at the national level. Finally, local administrations and communities will be involved not only in the monitoring of forested areas but also in the using the tools and practices introduced by the projects.

### Letter Content

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project <Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS FOR) in Kygyzstan> as included in the funding proposal submitted by Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) to us on June 1, Friday,2018.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the State Agency on Environment Protection and Forestry (SAEPF), the National Designated Authority of the Kyrgyz Republic.

Pursuant to GCF decision 8.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the project <Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS FOR) in Kyrgyzstan> as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The Government of the Kyrgyz Republic has no-objection to the project <Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS FOR) in Kyrgyzstan> as included in the funding proposal;  
(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the Kyrgyz Republic's national priorities, strategies and plans;  
(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the project <Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS FOR) in Kyrgyzstan> is considered acceptable.

# G  
CF/B.24/02/Add.02

## Secretariat’s assessment of FP116  

### Proposal name: Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands in Kyrgyz Republic (CS-FOR)  

- **Accredited entity:** Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)  
- **Country:** Kyrgyzstan  
- **Project size:** Small  

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat  

### 1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:  

#### Strengths  
- GCF grant financing will be used to mobilize further financing in forest and rangeland sectors, creating opportunities for scaling up.  
- Coordination and ownership among the different partners will be critical for successful implementation and to ensure sustainability.  
- An enabling environment for climate-related green investments will be strengthened and capacity-building for all relevant stakeholders provided.  
- Georeferenced monitoring will be used for the evaluation of forest-rangeland ecosystem management and will promote evidence-based planning, informing the business model for future replication and scaling up.  

#### Points of caution  
- Coordination and ownership among the different partners will be critical for successful implementation and to ensure sustainability.  
- An enabling environment for climate-related green investments will be strengthened and capacity-building for all relevant stakeholders provided.  
- Georeferenced monitoring will be used for the evaluation of forest-rangeland ecosystem management and will promote evidence-based planning, informing the business model for future replication and scaling up.  

### 2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XVII, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations”.  

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment  

### 2.1 Project background  

3. Kyrgyzstan is a low-income food-deficit country. Livestock is the most important source of income, the primary source of nutrition, and the financial safety net for the rural poor. Cropping is limited due to a lack of irrigation infrastructure and worsening climatic conditions – increasing temperatures and reducing the amount and duration of rainfalls. Climate change combined with poor management of natural resources have led to overexploitation and overgrazing by large groups of unproductive livestock, and to increasing forest degradation and unsustainable harvesting of timber from mountain slopes.  

4. The objective of the proposal is to enhance carbon sequestration and climate resilience of ecosystems and livelihoods. The project will store 19.8 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2eq) over 20 years. This represents an annual reduction of 990,000 tCO2eq and 7,920,000 tCO2eq over the eight years of project implementation. The total expected reduction

# GCF/B.24/02/Add.02  
## Page 110  

5. Aside from the activities at national scale, the project sites are in selected rural municipalities represented by the four contiguous districts of Ak-Talaa, Toguz-Toro, Suzak and Uzgen.  

6. The project seeks a GCF grant of approximatively USD 30 million, or 60 per cent of the total project cost. The project is classified as ESS category B. The project management unit costs USD 2.5 million, equivalent to 5 per cent of the project cost. This covers staff time, operational costs, and travels.  

## 2.2 Component-by-component analysis  

### Component 1: Evidence-based strengthening of natural resource management governance (total cost: USD 5.58; GCF cost: USD 5.18, or 92.8 per cent)  

7. This component aims to provide an enabling environment to support investments in forest-rangeland management through two approaches: 1) harmonization of relevant procedures and regulations for “climate-change sensitive” natural resource management, especially for tenure arrangements (output 1.1); and 2) the promotion of evidence-based and georeferenced monitoring and evaluation measures for natural resource and forest-rangeland ecosystem management (outputs 1.2 and 1.3).  

It aims to galvanize multi-stakeholder partnerships to promote technically sound integrated natural resource management in Kyrgyzstan. It also provides measures for capacity and organizational development – creating a national expert group for necessary consultations, providing technical support to pasture users unions and Leskhozes (local forest institutions) to update and enhance the needed data to implement climate-sensitive plans and strategies, and establishing community management groups to support work at landscape scale.  

8. The preservation of forest-rangeland ecosystems through the prevention of overgrazing and overharvesting of the forest/vegetative cover will be more effectively managed with the standardization of different legislation and full stakeholder inclusion. Through policy harmonization and capacity development at scale, the project will be able to incorporate climate risks in current management schemes in forest-rangeland ecosystems.  

### Component 2: Green investments for forest and rangeland rehabilitation (total cost: USD 22.40; GCF cost: USD 20.30, or 91 per cent)  

9. This component aims to contribute to community resilience through capacity development of key stakeholders in natural resource management to promote sustainable practices like rotational grazing, to enhance monitoring capacities, and to develop and implement integrated natural resource management and climate resilience plans (output 2.1).  

This is combined with investments to restore and improve forest-rangelands (output 2.2) in the project sites through key interventions: afforestation, reforestation, forest enrichment, pasture rehabilitation and livestock investment.  

10. Through an integrated approach, stakeholders will be incentivized to include climate risks in forest-rangeland planning and management. The skills needed for proper maintenance of the investments will be strengthened (e.g. fire protection, pest management and monitoring), which will ensure ownership and sustainability post-project.  

### Component 3: Climate-sensitive value chain development (total cost: USD 18.01; GCF cost: USD 3.01, or 16.8 per cent)  

11. This component aims to incentivize sustainable investments in carbon sequestration. Activities include market assessments, certifications, awareness campaigns on marketing.

# GC

## 2.2.1. Overall technical assessment

12. In summary, the project’s mitigation and adaptation goals are focused on integrated rangeland and forestry management guided by the prevention of natural resource degradation and enhancement of ecosystem services; multi-stakeholder engagement with emphasis on communities and the private sector; promotion of bottom-up activities with local communities; and the introduction of market-driven incentives for resource users to adopt and maintain management practices that sequester and preserve stored carbon. All of which will be supported by an enabling environment with coherent underlying policies and by the strengthening of key national and local institutions and planning processes, which overall will improve governance of forest and rangeland in Kyrgyzstan.

13. There is a strong connectivity between the three components. Components 1 and 2 provide for the creation of an enabling environment, baseline data sets, and capacity combined with the restoration and rehabilitation of forests and rangelands. Component 3 provides the scale and sustainability by promoting investments in the natural resources sector in Kyrgyzstan. The project’s paradigm shift could be adapted and tested in other districts and neighbouring countries.

## III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 3.1 Impact potential    Scale: High

14. Under business as usual (substantial degradation trend), the level of forest degradation would generate an estimated net loss of 1.5 MtCO2eq in 20 years. The project will be able to avoid the mentioned losses and to generate an additional 18 MtCO2eq sequestration (15.1 MtCO2eq from rangeland and livestock rehabilitation, 2.9 MtCO2eq from forest activities and 0.2 MtCO2eq from other agricultural activities), for a net effect of 19.8 MtCO2eq sequestered.

15. The project will support the reduction of emissions and enhance carbon storage of about 19.8 MtCO2eq through: (i) the creation of legal and enabling environments supported by an innovative evidence-based climate and natural resource planning and monitoring system; (ii) community-based investments in natural forest regeneration, sustainable forest management, afforestation and reforestation; (iii) the rehabilitation of rangelands and prevention of further degradation; (iv) the diversification of options for community livelihoods (i.e. income portfolios); and (v) the reduction of emission intensity per unit of animal protein (meat and milk).

16. Sequestration of 19.8 MtCO2eq in 20 years represents 7.6 per cent of the country’s total emissions, and 22.6 per cent of the agricultural share of emissions. The cost per tCO2eq is USD 2.5.

17. Average annual temperatures have increased during the period 1960–2010 (0.025 oC/year) with a higher rate of increase in the period 1990–2010 (0.07 oC/year), with the greatest rate of warming occurring during the winter months. Annual precipitation has shown a slight downward trend in the last 20 years (–1.868 mm/year). These small but significant changes in temperature and rainfall are affecting ecosystems and ecosystem services, and thus community livelihoods, which depend on natural resources for their living.

18. The climate resilience of 432,450 individuals (90,000 households), including 246,497  
women in Leskhozes, national parks, local self-government bodies, community organizations,  
the private sector in the high climate-risk area will be improved, representing 9.8 per cent of the  
country’s total population.  

19. As part of a resilience impact measurement analysis methodology, developed by the  
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), the resilience capacity index  
has been calculated for the target and control group. For both, a high education level and the  
diversification of income portfolios are the main variables of adaptive capacity. This finding  
confirms the need for economic diversification as part of building resilience. The project will  
increase the resilience of beneficiaries by reducing their dependency on direct uses of natural  
resources, by improving ecosystems functions, and by diversifying livelihoods and income  
sources.  

20. An estimated 3,000 ha of dry, high-altitude deforested or severely degraded forest lands  
will be restored through natural forest enrichment practices. In addition, 56,000 ha of forest,  
and 644,000 ha of rangelands will be under improved management. An additional 20 per cent of  
producers will use carbon sequestration technologies and practices.  

21. An analysis of the land productivity dynamics via the FAO Earth Map will allow the  
assessment of project’s positive impacts on ecosystems.  

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential                          Scale: High  

22. The project aims to contribute to shifting national agricultural production from a  
predominantly unsustainable subsistence livestock production impacting on the carbon storage  
of forests and rangelands to a diversified and climate-sensitive value chain business-oriented  
economy where investments in mitigation enhance the resilience of ecosystems and  
communities by providing ecosystem services (e.g. improved climate regulation, flood  
regulation, soil retention and habitat provision).  

23. The major constraint on the increase of carbon sequestration in forests and rangelands  
has been the traditional extensive grazing practices and expansion of pasture areas. The project  
aims to provide incentives for communities to preserve and expand forested areas and to better  
manage grazing lands through improved management practices and establishing linkages with  
markets leading to diversified and more profitable agriculture for beneficiaries.  

24. As diversification of income sources is demonstrated as the main driver of adaptive  
capacity, the project will reinforce policies, legal frameworks and strategies enabling  
diversification through improved forest and grassland management. Coordination will be  
reinforced among these core planning frameworks and governance structures. The more  
conducive policy and institutional environment will ensure sustainability and facilitate the  
replication and scale up of future investments.  

25. Integrated natural resources climate resilient management plans will be developed with  
local governments, pasture committees, communities and the private sector. Improved  
governance is important for private sector investments into afforestation and reforestation.  

26. The project through training sessions, engagement workshops, awareness campaigns,  
media outreaches, and knowledge-sharing events will also strengthen knowledge generation  
and dissemination of good practices throughout the country, including to young people.  
Moreover, the tools used, and skills strengthened will ensure that climate change is taken into  
account in future decision-making by the communities, public institutions and private sector. A  
dedicated communication and knowledge management specialist will be assigned to the project  
implementation team.

## 27. The project will inform the national institutional and legal framework by providing climate and gender-sensitive evidences and lessons learned, which will provide for long-term transformational change. It will also integrate climate risk management into the national and subnational planning processes. Moreover, an improved methodology to monitor and assess pasture and forest degradation will be developed and used to generate the evidences from geospatial data.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential                      Scale: High  

## 28. Community mobilization and training sessions will be undertaken in 50 municipalities and will include social mobilization, gender training, institutional support, and the establishment of task forces and fire management teams. Women and young people will take part in the selection of the project-supported value chains and in the capacity-building activities. Access for women to improved natural resources (forests and rangelands) will be encouraged to ensure equitable distribution of benefits. The investments under the project will generate up to 3,300 new jobs.

## 29. In addition to contributing to carbon sequestration, afforestation/reforestation and forest-enrichment activities will improve soil fertility and stability, reduce land degradation, improve soil water retention and enrich biodiversity. Plant diversity will also be improved under pasture rotation. The expected carbon sequestration will increase the availability of drinking water and of non-timber forest products, which were valued in the economic analysis. Moreover, climate change mitigation and biodiversity issues will be harmonized across strategies and legislation through the planned policy dialogue.

## 30. Better herd management will contribute to an expected rise of livestock production by at least 10 per cent (under the assumption of an increasing share of cows and a decreasing livestock herd number by 20 per cent). Individual average financial benefits are positive from the selected value chains (internal rates of return between 22 and 39 per cent and net present values between USD 5,800 and 21,400 per ha), but the highest benefits are when economic benefits are accounted for from ecosystem services. Also, forest users will benefit from collecting non-timber forest products.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient              Scale: High  

## 31. Most of the population lives in the valleys and foothills of the mountains, where vulnerability to climate events is particularly high and where around 50–80 per cent of agricultural land are affected by land degradation. Food insecurity is exacerbated by climate-related shocks, including floods and mudslides. Thirteen per cent of children suffer from malnutrition, and around 40 per cent of children and women suffer from anaemia due to micronutrient deficiencies.

## 32. Few investments are in the forest sector. Rangeland investments have received more attention from donors, but the focus was strictly on livelihoods and not on including carbon sequestration, which makes a significant contribution to reducing GHG emissions in the country, as explained in the country’s nationally determined contribution.

### 3.5 Country ownership                       Scale: High  

## 33. The Government of Kyrgyzstan includes ecosystem-based adaptation in its “Climate change adaptation programme and action plan for 2015–2017 for the forest and biodiversity sector”. Mitigation and adaptation actions in the sector are included in the nationally determined contribution. FAO will contribute to the national adaptation planning process by

The project was designed with key governmental agencies, coordination bodies, and communities. The main stakeholders identified the project target areas and their roles and responsibilities in project implementation. This has created a strong sense of ownership of the project, which will contribute to the project performance and sustainability. The private sector was also engaged in designing the investments in the key value chains and in the identification of target areas and priority expansion areas.

Key stakeholders have been part of the project design. In the embodiment of the project steering committee, they will continue to be involved in project implementation from planning to monitoring. It is expected that the project activities will be included in the government’s regular annual work plans. The State Agency for Environment Protection and Forestry (SAEPF), the lead governmental body for climate change, acts as the secretariat of the Climate Change Coordination Commission (headed by the First Vice-Prime Minister of the Kyrgyz Republic) and is one executing entity (EE) in this project, a member of the steering committee and hosting the project management unit. Other members of the steering committee include the Ministry of Agriculture, Food Industry and Melioration, Community Development and Investment Agency (ARIS), RKDF, Climate Finance Center (CFC), Ministry of Emergency Situations (MES), State Agency for Local Self Government and Interethnic Relations (SALSGIER), and State Registration Agency.

The approach for integrated forest and pasture management will be based on a set of principles, which are reinforcing country ownership: transparency, participation, accountability, fairness/equity, coordination and capacity development. Community consultative groups will be established at the local level (watershed or forest enterprise level) to participate in the development of integrated natural resources management and climate resilient plans. Recommendations for tenure arrangements aiming at improving access and sustainable use will be developed.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness  
#### Scale: High  

The level of concessionality is justified by the substantial expected mitigation impact coupled with the limited financial return of investment in forests, and the high transaction costs associated to mobilizing different stakeholders i.e. government agencies, local self-government bodies, Leskhozes, and Pasture Use Unions for joint investments in rangeland rehabilitation, which otherwise would not make the investment attractive for the private sector.

Component 3 will ensure the sustainability of the investments in carbon sequestration carried out in component 2 and will create economic opportunities to decrease degradation of natural resources in the project areas, which will enhance the resilience of target communities. All private investments generated under component 3 will be undertaken entirely with private funds and not GCF funding, aside from the capacity development support activities.

The estimated benefits in terms of sequestration is 19.8 MtCO2eq, with the total investment of USD 2.5 per tCO2eq. The project utilizes investment of USD 115 per direct beneficiary, and USD 51 per total beneficiary (direct and indirect). The average economic benefit per direct beneficiary in the project area is estimated at approximately USD 41.1 per year (economic net present value of the operation per beneficiary per year), as a result of the project interventions.

Overall, GCF grant financing is appropriate for this project given the public nature and low financial returns of the GCF-funded investments. Component 1 provides public goods in the form of policy and legal framework, monitoring and evaluation systems, and planning and monitoring. GCF funds under components 2 and 3 will go toward capacity-building, which.

## IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  

### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards  

#### 4.1.1. Environmental and social risk category  

42. The accredited entity (AE) has carried out an initial risk assessment against its environmental and social safeguards and considered the project to be of moderate risk. Potential environmental and social risks and impacts identified are not considered major, will not extend beyond the area of influence of the project and are neither irreversible nor cumulative. The Secretariat confirms the AE’s environmental and social risk category assessment, which is equivalent to category B.

#### 4.1.2. Safeguard instrument and information disclosure  

43. Since exact subprojects are not determined yet and will be decided during project implementation based on demand and consultations with the concerned communities, the AE has prepared an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) as the platform to guide the identification and management of potential negative environmental and social impacts of proposed activities. The ESMF is prepared in compliance with the environmental and social management guidelines of the AE, taking into consideration the GCF’s environmental and social safeguard requirements. The ESMF will be disclosed on the AE’s and GCF’s websites.

44. The ESMF discussed the legal and institutional framework and provided an overview of environmental, climate and social considerations in the country and in the target areas. The procedures for identifying and risk-screening procedures for subprojects are also presented, including the preparation, approval, and execution and determination of potential impacts and mitigation measures. While a detailed environmental and social assessment is not envisaged for the activities, all subprojects will be further assessed against the FAO screening criteria for environmental and social management. Should the activities require further environmental assessment by FAO or by the national regulators, these will be conducted. The FAO Environmental and Social Screening checklist will be used to determine if an environmental and social management plan is needed for each subproject. The nature, magnitude, reversibility and location of impacts are the main elements to consider in the screening of subprojects and will be complemented by expert judgement. National environmental impact assessment legislation requirements will also be referred to. The project will not fund or be involved in subprojects/investments rated as high risk, and it has developed an exclusion list that will not be supported by the project.

# GCF/B.24/02/Add.02  
## Page 116  

The project aims to mitigate climate change through increased carbon sequestration and adaptive strategies to enhance climate resilience. Potential environmental and social risks and impacts of the activities under the project relate to soil conservation; agricultural inputs and fertilizer use; crop residues and solid waste; pasture and livestock management; water management; pest and pesticide use and management; and ecosystems and biodiversity concerns.

One of the investment activities under the project includes provision of loans to eligible value chain actors through the RKDF and participating commercial banks. These loans may be for packaging equipment, laboratory equipment, vacuum and solar driers, agricultural machinery, greenhouses, nurseries for tree plantations and commercial orchards, eco-tourism, and agricultural processing facilities, among others.

The ESMF provides for possible avoidance and mitigation measures to manage the identified potential risks and impact of the subprojects. It also provides for the preparation of a detailed environmental and social commitment plan that will incorporate the recommended mitigations of the environmental and social assessment as well as the inputs from the stakeholder engagement process. The project’s interventions and the corresponding management measures will be implemented within the context of the integrated natural resource management and climate resilience plans developed through the community landscape management groups.

The AE confirms that there are no communities over which the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy applies, and the project will not affect cultural heritage. Nevertheless, with Kyrgyz pastoralists as a key community stakeholder, the ESMF sets out certain safeguards for the communities, including that voluntary agreement by communities for any temporary/managed disruption to access to areas of land/natural resources must be obtained. Creating synergies with FAO networks, such as the Pastoralist Knowledge Hub, could also provide capacity-building opportunities for stakeholders around pastoralist issues.

### 4.1.4. Institutional arrangements  

46. The project will establish a project implementation unit (PIU), which will be responsible for the daily management of operations. Among others, a safeguards specialist is expected to be based within the PIU. The safeguards specialist is tasked with ensuring overall compliance and supporting the safeguard performance monitoring throughout the life of the project including all aspects of environmental and social safeguard, grievance redress, stakeholder engagement, reporting, coordinating and supervising subproject screening and related environmental and social management plan preparation and execution.

The project will also provide capacity building activities as regards sustainable forest and rangeland management to the involved institutions and communities.

### 4.1.5. Stakeholder engagement, information disclosure and grievance redress mechanism  

47. Stakeholder consultations with various stakeholders at both national and local levels have been conducted during project formulation. It is also planned for stakeholder engagement to continue during project implementation through the community landscape management groups and various local and national stakeholders and user groups in the development of the integrated natural resource management and climate resilience plans.

The community landscape management groups are informal institutions that, which will be established at the local level by the project to advance the participatory management of resources. The AE will undertake disclosure for all moderate risk projects and will establish a disclosure portal to publicly disclose project documentation, including on environmental and social safeguards.

Institutional, project-level and internal grievance redress mechanism structures are established and time frames to resolve issues and contact details through which to lodge grievances are provided.

48. The AE has submitted a gender assessment; therefore, it complies with the operational guidelines of the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan. The gender assessment describes the context of gender issues at the country level, giving reference to the national policy environment for promoting gender equality in Kyrgyzstan. It also describes beliefs that shape gender roles and perpetuate gender inequality, which disproportionately affect women. Furthermore, the gender assessment provides information on gender issues in the forestry and agricultural sectors, such as the limited access to resources for women and their limited opportunities to participate in decision-making platforms, including how this affects their needs and priorities being ensured.

49. For example, many activities have a timeline of by end of year 7 and 8, these are too stretched for a project that has an implementation period of 8 years. The project will promote certification of local products, such as non-timber forest products, and there is potential for women to benefit from this. The gender assessment also identifies certification of local products in the gender action plan as an important source of income and household consumption for families living near forests, particularly for female-headed households and poor households. ## 4.3 Risks  

### 4.3.1. Overall programme assessment (medium)  

55. GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 29.9 million, which will lead to the sequestration of 19.8 MtCO2eq in 20 years by investing in rangeland and forestry management in Kyrgyzstan. The Government agencies, the AE and beneficiaries are providing co-financing of USD 5 million as in-kind contributions and the RKDF is providing a senior loan of USD 15 million.  

### 4.3.2. Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the project (medium)  

56. FAO will serve both as the AE and co-EE for the project. The AE has funded over USD 600 million for projects supporting countries in the field of climate change and disaster risk management. It has collaborated with Kyrgyzstan since 1993 and a country office was opened in 2009. The country office currently manages 18 projects in the country. It has also been providing technical support to investments from other agencies, such as the World Bank and the International Fund for Agricultural Development.  

57. In addition to FAO, there are three more EEs, namely SAEPF, ARIS, and RKDF. The SAEPF is the National Designated Authority, implementing policy related to environmental protection and the sustainable use of natural resources. It has a track record of managing a number of projects, including a forest management project funded by external donors. ARIS is a non-commercial organization investing local priority issues and will implement specific activities of this project. The RKDF has funded 925 enterprises for a total amount over USD 275 million as of April 2018. It will provide a credit line via five commercial banks in this project. It also has experience with the International Fund for Agricultural Development in providing loans to agriculture producers and processers. All EEs are providing co-financing by way of in-kind finance or loans. There is a need for effective coordination among the AE and EEs.  

### 4.3.3. Programme-specific execution risks (medium)  

58. The funding proposal points out that the existing regulatory framework fails to support sustainable natural resources management, including the sustainable impact of carbon sequestration. For example, there is a lack of legal framework in carbon pricing initiatives, carbon emission reduction rights, ownership and retirement in the country. As the key returns of project investment are represented by carbon sequestration, this may make the project investment unattractive for investors. To mitigate the risk, the project will mainstream climate change and effective natural resources management at the policy level.  

59. At the investment level, it will invest in non-commercial tree species in state-owned forest areas where forests are established and maintained in order to ensure ecosystem services without specific direct economic purposes. The project will also provide incentives for forest protection via the provision of alternative livelihoods. Given the growth in rural energy consumption, the project will focus on promoting sustainable energy practices in rural areas.

# GCF/B.24/02/Add.02  
## Page 119  

The project will try to attract private sector investment in forestry and rangeland protection activities. It will enable private sector engagement by enhancing the inclusive policy-level dialogue and establishing stakeholder engagement process. However, afforestation/reforestation activities are unattractive for private sector participation: most of the non-commercial local varieties in forestry investments generate no or minimal financial returns but do generate ecological co-benefits at a 20-year horizon.  

As for rangeland investments activities, improvement of rangelands conditions will contribute over 70 per cent of the carbon sequestration potential of the project. However, rangelands are the centre of the livelihoods of rural communities in the target areas, thus, it needs incentives to change the behaviour of the private sector to invest in the activities that reduce the pressure of using rangelands in livelihood activities.  

To attract private sector investment in both forestry and rangeland protection activities, the project will provide different levels of concessionality depending on the return profiles of the investment. RKDF provides concessional co-financing for these activities.  

The AE has identified the risk that expected disbursement rates by RKDF and partner banks may not be reached due to the saturation of the agriculture lending market. According to the International Monetary Fund report, the agriculture sector accounts for the top second (18.5 per cent) of the credit portfolio of banks and the first sector (28.3 percent) of microfinance organization. The national bank also encourages commercial banks to participate in the state programmes related to financing agriculture.  

The AE stated that the project in partnership with the national bank will explore new opportunities in agriculture lending for product development in cooperation with interested financial institutions. The AE responded that the capacity-building component could also serve as an incentive for the banks to reach the project target groups.  

## Financial and economic analysis  

The project provides disaggregated accounting of the financial and economic returns by type of activity and by component. While the financial returns in afforestation/reforestation are negative in 10 years and barely positive (financial internal rate of return of 11.1 per cent) in 20 years. The economic returns are expected to be higher (economic internal rate of return of 71.3 per cent) as it takes into account the valuation of carbon sequestration (USD 40/tCO2eq) and the ecosystem benefits.  

It also provides sensitivity analysis considering variations in climate change affecting costs and benefits, changes in potential sequestration and valuation, and also in implementation. Overall, the sensitivity analysis estimates economic internal rates of return ranging from 27.1 to 112.4 per cent under different scenarios. It is highly sensitive to the one to two-year delay in project activities, which emphasizes the importance of the timeliness implementation of the initial phase of the project.

The AE has reaffirmed that it will apply its internal anti-money-laundering/countering the financing of terrorism policies to the project as required by the accreditation master agreement (AMA). Based on the information provided, Compliance assigns a medium compliance risk to the project.

74. The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 8 June 2018, and it became effective on 4 October 2018.

Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

## Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP116  
### Proposal name: Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS-FOR) in the Kyrgyz Republic  
### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)  
### Project/programme size: Small  

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  

### 1.1 Impact potential           Scale: High  

#### 1.1.1. General background  

Kyrgyzstan has been classified as a low-income, food-deficit country. Two-thirds of the country’s 6 million people live in rural areas of the country. Agriculture is the main source of livelihood, with livestock the leading source of income and primary source of nutrition; it also provides a financial safety net for the rural poor. Crop production has not been thriving well in the country due to a combination of inadequate irrigation infrastructure and the negative impact on productivity of worsening climate conditions.  

As fully articulated in the funding proposal and some of the annexes, poor management of resources and negative climate change impacts are causing overexploitation and overgrazing of large groups of unproductive livestock, increasing forest degradation and unsustainable harvesting of timber from mountain slopes.  

The interactions between changing weather patterns, the impact of these changes on communities and forests, and the degradation of natural resource systems due to human activities has led to an increase in damage from extreme weather events and a growing threat to the future provisioning of key ecosystem services.  

The Government of Kyrgyzstan recognizes the need to take action to halt this trend, as is evident from the key interventions for investments in rangeland and forest management, which are well elucidated in the country’s intended nationally determined contribution (INDC). The interventions will commit the country to reducing, by 2050, its greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by 35 to 37 per cent with international assistance.  

The proposed project has been designed to assist the country in meeting these commitments. The sequestration objective, which is well entrenched in the proposed strategy of the project, capitalizes on important co-benefits from adaptation and disaster risk reduction and is expected to be achieved through the following project components:  

#### (a) Component 1: evidence-based strengthening of natural resources management (NRM) governance  

This component will support the Government’s ongoing efforts to harmonize relevant policies and legal frameworks, and strengthen its planning, monitoring and evaluation systems.  

#### (b) Component 2: green investments for forests and rangeland rehabilitation  

This component will introduce a process of local integrated rangeland and forestry resource planning built around forest, rangeland and livestock management practices. This will sequester carbon and is expected to be responsive to observed changes in the climate and enable the diversification of household income earning activities; and  

#### (c) Component 3: climate-sensitive value chains development  

This will facilitate market opportunities that provide the financial incentives, funded by the Russian Kyrgyz

4. Both the climate change mitigation and adaptation needs of the country are adequately justified in this project. First and foremost, reducing GHG emissions in all countries is a very important contribution to the contribution to fulfilling the goals of the Paris Agreement.  

Kyrgyzstan’s INDC under the Paris Agreement commits the country to reducing its GHG emissions by 35 to 37 per cent by 2050 through international assistance. Although the document did not produce clear strategies to achieve the envisaged GHG emission reductions, it emphasized that forestry and land use/land use changes appear to be among the most relevant sectors to target in the country to secure both adaptation and mitigation targets.  

In order to structure and enrich the mitigation target interventions, FAO, as requested by the Government of Kyrgyzstan, began the process of developing a proposal for the project “Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands in Kyrgyzstan (CS-FOR).” In developing this project, FAO ensured that the climate change mitigation needs of the country were adequately and sufficiently built into the project development by using inputs from climate change and climate rationale developed earlier for the country. Many of the inputs came from documents already submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), given that the inputs for the design of this project were taken from government sector planning documents, including those prepared as part of the national climate change mitigation and adaptation plans (e.g. the INDC, Third National Communication (TNC), etc.).

The project objectives, which were clearly elucidated in this report, include:

# GCF/B.24/02/Add.02  
Page 124  

## Part 1: Project Intervention and Carbon Sequestration

The project intervention is designed to adopt a participatory approach, which is adaptive to climate change and responsive to the needs of local communities.

### Key Expected Targets and Coverage

The key expected targets and coverage of this project intervention include carbon sequestration through investment in rangeland and forest management. Achievement of the carbon sequestration results is expected to lead to the capitalization of important co-benefits from adaptation and disaster risk reduction by supporting the Government’s ongoing efforts to harmonize relevant policies and legal frameworks, and strengthen its planning, monitoring and evaluation systems.

Carbon sequestration will be achieved through the introduction by this project intervention of a process involving local integrated rangeland and forestry resource planning built around forest, rangeland and livestock management practices. The process is designed to be responsive to observed changes in the climate and is expected to enable the diversification of household income-earning activities as part of the process.

This process will engender market opportunities that will provide financial incentives for resource users to adopt and maintain management practices that sequester and preserve stored carbon. The financial incentives will be funded by a co-financing senior loan from RKDF.

### Alignment with GCF Performance Measurement Framework

Achievement of the project targets and coverage discussed above are well and strongly aligned with the following GCF performance measurement framework indicators: impact potential; country ownership; and needs of the recipients/beneficiaries. The alignment with the other metrics, including paradigm shift, sustainable development potential, and efficiency and effectiveness, can be described as strongly medium or weakly high for reasons that will be discussed later in this assessment.

## Part 2: Project Intervention Details

### Estimated Carbon Sequestration

From the information provided in the funding proposal and some of the annexes, it can be concluded that the estimated target in terms of the carbon that will eventually be sequestered by the project intervention will be about 19.8 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2eq) and will be delivered by the following interventions:

1. The creation of legal and management enabling environments supported by an innovative evidence-based climate and natural resource planning and monitoring system;
2. Community-based investments in natural forest regeneration, sustainable forest management, afforestation and reforestation;
3. Rehabilitation of rangelands and prevention of further degradation;
4. Diversification of options for community livelihoods; and
5. Reduction of emission intensity per unit of animal protein.

### Carbon Accounting Methodology

The carbon emissions reductions that will be delivered by the sequestration activities listed above have been accurately estimated using appropriate methodologies, which were well presented in annex 3(a) to the funding proposal titled “CS-FOR – Carbon Accounting”.

It is also estimated that a total of 432,450 individuals, including 246,497 females, will be direct and indirect beneficiaries of this intervention. The approach used for this estimation, which is based on the RIMA II methodology of FAO, is found to be appropriate for the analysis, which formed the basis of the estimation of direct and indirect beneficiaries.

The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) therefore concludes that the number of beneficiaries as a core indicator of impacts is accurately estimated and calculation methodologies correctly provided.

## Part 3: Climate Change Vulnerability

### Project Design Focus

With regard to climate change vulnerability, the project design actually focused on a carefully selected locality. The chosen intervention areas were explicitly discussed in detail in chapter 1 of the feasibility study.

Analyses of the study area, consisting of four districts of the country, related to the extent and status of rangeland and forest resources as well as the identification of changes in key climate parameters and related hazards; these were presented in annex 6(b) to the funding proposal titled “Project Atlas”.

The four target districts are said to be the most vulnerable to climate-related stresses and hazards. In these four districts, green investment interventions for forest and rangeland rehabilitation and livestock management will be implemented.

The critical results expected to be delivered by this project will include: carbon sequestration from improved forest and rangeland management; livelihood of people in forest productivity activities; and better livestock management activities. Adaptive capacity strengthening is also expected to be delivered by component 1 of this project, which will strengthen ongoing efforts of the Government of Kyrgyzstan to harmonize relevant policies and legal frameworks as well as strengthen its planning, monitoring and evaluation systems. All these critical results will be achieved through different levels of impacts during the period of project implementation. For example, while reinforcement of government policies in adaptive capacity strengthening will start to manifest during the early stages of the intervention, carbon sequestration delivery may start to occur during the latter part of implementation due to the longer time needed for forested vegetative growth to occur.

### 1.1.4. Potential mitigation/adaptation impacts and degree of lock-in infrastructure  

10. This project intervention covers both mitigation and adaptation responses to climate change in Kyrgyzstan. Climate change mitigation and adaptation are priority areas clearly articulated in the INDC of Kyrgyzstan that was submitted to the UNFCCC secretariat in 2015. The country’s INDC emphasized the importance of mitigation and adaptation as priority areas for Kyrgyzstan, especially as they are linked to the risks to people’s livelihoods, the environment, the national economy, and achieving wider Sustainable Development Goals. National climate change policies and action plans have also been developed in Kyrgyzstan for various sectors of the country’s economy, including: emergency situations; biodiversity and forestry; agriculture; and water management.  

Climate change is also addressed in other national responses to international policy, including the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification (UNCCD) and the United Nations Convention on Biological Diversity. The national action plan and its activity frameworks for implementing the UNCCD in the Kyrgyzstan for 2015−2020 have many highly relevant actions on land degradation that are directly linked to climate change adaptation measures for the agricultural and livestock sectors, and particularly for pasturelands.  

The national action plan highlights that strengthening the capacity for state management of land resources, effective land-use policies, and achievement of sustainable use of land resources will be necessary; it states that a principal condition is to maintain and increase the potential productivity of land while maintaining vital ecosystem functions of soil. While most of these policy documents stress adaptation intervention, mitigating intervention in the form of carbon sequestration was much emphasized in another national document, the “Third National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan”, a key emphasis of this project.  

Thus the information provided in the funding proposal and the feasibility study utilized much of the important information in these national documents to highlight the fact that the interventions foreseen by this project are the most feasible and suitable mitigation and adaptation options for Kyrgyzstan.

In the absence of this project intervention, unsustainable grazing will continue, causing the expansion of pasture areas, and exacerbated by the continued use of traditional extensive grazing practices, with negative impacts on the protection of forest cover. ## 1.2.2. Potential for knowledge and learning

Knowledge management and capacity-building for key stakeholders represent innovative solutions that will be delivered by the proposed project. Project stakeholders will not only gain new capacities and knowledge about the different interventions, but they will also gain tools and skills that will support them to factor climate change into their decision-making processes and livelihood strategies. The project will therefore deliver a strong platform for knowledge enhancement and learning for stakeholders.

The interventions are innovative for Kyrgyzstan, especially when the national efforts to mainstream climate change into the project's key intervention is considered. The project will deliver various innovative interventions: capacity-building of stakeholders to mainstream climate change considerations into their normal activities; development of market mechanisms for their livelihood; and improved farming and animal husbandry, among others. It can therefore be concluded that the type of investment proposed will support innovative interventions in Kyrgyzstan.

## 1.2.3. Enabling environment/sustainability of outcomes

The plan described in the funding proposal is well structured, covering: identification of the regions and sites where the chosen interventions will be carried out; development of an enabling environment for the governance and management of the selected interventions; and development of an enabling environment for the sustainable development and replication of the outcome. The feasibility study (annex 2 of the funding proposal) also describes these features of the project.

It can also be argued that the use of grant funding from GCF, blended with co-financing in the form of a senior loan, will not only provide funds for the private sector and other beneficiaries whose livelihoods in sustainable agriculture will be enhanced, but it can also offer a very robust financial exit strategy.

As presented in the funding proposal and its annexes, the project is planned to bring about very explicit behavioural changes in institutions, communities and groups. Component 1 has been designed to strengthen the Government's ongoing efforts to harmonize relevant policies and legal frameworks, and to strengthen its planning, monitoring and evaluation systems. This strategy is based on the awareness that a key measure in preserving the natural resources of the forest and rangeland ecosystem in Kyrgyzstan is to improve governance related to their management. Improved management will prevent livestock overgrazing, overharvesting of fuelwood and timber, will create an enabling environment that stimulates innovative practices and investments, and conserve and regenerate these resources.

26. It has been recognized that to achieve these goals there will need to be a change in the outlook of existing institutional management and the regulation of forest and rangelands. Not only will the capacity of existing institutions be repositioned to utilize modern NRM principles, but the capacity of their staff to understand climate change risks and NRM will be improved together with their skills and capacity to monitor and regulate the use of forest and rangeland. (a) Capacity development and mobilization of an expert group for technical assistance. The project will select personnel from local research and outreach organizations who will be trained on policy-making and management of natural resources. The expert group will include a gender and social development specialist, and the terms of reference for each member will ensure gender mainstreaming. Key activities for which the capacity of the expert group will be built and strengthened by the project will include: preparing communication materials and organizing information awareness campaigns to mobilize national stakeholders; organizing international conferences and meetings to sensitize stakeholders; organizing training sessions and workshops on forest and rangeland tenure arrangements, policy-making, and management of natural resources; proposing recommendations for enforcement of sustainable management and use of forest and rangeland ecosystems through participatory processes; identifying approaches for national stakeholder involvement processes; organizing a national stakeholders’ platform for policy dialogue for the management and use of municipal forest; and facilitating thematic workshops, and submitting the recommendation document to parliament; and  

(b) The capacities of monitoring groups at the central level of the State Agency for Environmental Protection and Forestry (SAEPF) and ARIS (the executing entities (EEs) for the project with FAO) will be built and/or strengthened by this project intervention. Key outputs of the capacity-building activities included in component 1 of this project will include enhanced capacities on: climate change risks and natural resource assessments; monitoring and evaluation with evidence-based integrated NRM planning, monitoring and evaluation tools and methodologies; facilitating linkages across evidence, primary data, information systems and the forest-rangeland ecosystem planning processes; and using a geo-referencing and evidence-based approach.

## 1.2.4. Replicability and scalability/knowledge and learning  

29. The potential for replicability and scaling up of the successes of this project intervention is presented in the funding proposal and annexes and can be summarized as follows:  

(a) The harmonization of national policies and strategies, which will be carried out as part of component 1 of the project, will not only benefit sound project delivery in the target areas but also provide a framework for replication and scaling up across the country.

## 1.4 Needs of the recipient      Scale: High  

## 1.4.1. Economic and social needs and climate vulnerability  

### 41. According to information presented in the funding proposal and its annexes and from open literature sources, it is clear that the geography and topography of Kyrgyzstan render it highly susceptible to hazards and disasters, which are becoming more frequent due to climate change. These hazards and disasters include: drought, land and mudslides, avalanches, violent winds, downpours, ice and frosts, breakthrough of glacial lakes, floods, river erosion, and earthquakes. A large proportion of the Kyrgyzstan population lives in the valleys and foothills of the mountains, where vulnerability to these events is particularly high and losses are extremely high.  

### 42. Target beneficiaries and their productive assets in the project’s target regions, which are those deemed to be the most affected, are exposed to these risks. It has also been established by some of the studies on which the intervention strategy was based that many of these disasters, and especially their effects, are exacerbated by unsustainable management of forests, rangelands and livestock in the country. The proposed project therefore aims at delivering more robust resource management for forests, rangelands and livestock to reduce the negative effects of these disasters. It can therefore be concluded that given the economic, social and climate vulnerability the project interventions are necessary in Kyrgyzstan.  

## 1.4.2. Financial needs  

### 43. Kyrgyzstan is a low-income food deficit country with a population of nearly six million, of which two-thirds live in rural areas. Agriculture is one of the key economic sectors in Kyrgyzstan, with 48 per cent of the labour force dedicated to this sector, which represents almost 15 per cent of gross domestic product. As a result, the country does not have the national financial capacity to address the problems targeted by this project from its own resources due to the limited nature of national and local resources.  

### 44. The kinds of interventions required cannot attract funding from the very weak domestic private finance entities, nor can they attract financing from international sources, as returns are low and the payback period is indefinite for such investment in many cases, especially where the intervention will not deliver revenue streams. GCF grant funding is therefore needed to overcome the financial barriers. The grant funding from GCF will also strengthen the funds from RKDF, a private sector entity that will provide its contributions as wholesale credits to local

### 1.4.3. Institutional needs  

45. According to information presented in the funding proposal, institutions in Kyrgyzstan involved with management, regulation and policy setting for forests, rangelands and livestock lack the strong integrated policy and legal framework needed to deliver public-private partnerships in integrated natural resources management. Consequently, the capacities of institutions and of other relevant stakeholders will be built and/or strengthened by this intervention.  

For government institutions, areas that will be strengthened will include: policy and regulatory capabilities, especially targeted at forest, rangeland and livestock management; monitoring, reporting and verification capabilities for ecosystem-based interventions; low-carbon production systems in forestry and agriculture, including livestock sectors, of the Kyrgyzstan economy.  

For non-government stakeholders, the areas of strengthening will range from ecosystem-based entrepreneurial activities, especially those that will deliver carbon sequestration opportunities and climate resilient livelihoods, to the financing of such activities.  

46. The iTAP ranked the potential for this proposed project intervention to meet the needs of the recipients to be high.

This ensures an alignment of the CS-FOR project design and implementation with the climate policies of Kyrgyzstan; and  

(d) It is well articulated in the funding proposal that the project conforms to and is aligned with all national climate change strategies and action plans developed for various sectors, including emergency situations, biodiversity and forestry, and agriculture and water management.

## 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness         Scale: High  

### 1.6.1. Financial structure  

### 63. Of the total funding needed to implement this project (USD 49.99 million), USD 29.99 million has been requested from GCF as grant funding. This funding request from GCF has been justified in the funding proposal according to the following reasons:  

(a) Grant funding requested is justified given the substantial expected impact in contribution to sequester about 19.8 MtCO2eq through forest and rangeland investment; and  

(b) In the absence of GCF funding, joint investment in rangeland rehabilitation such as planned in this project will result in limited or negative financial return of investment in forests. This will be due to the high cost of funds from other sources, when and if they can be mobilized, the short-term nature of such funds and the high transaction costs associated with mobilizing different stakeholders for joint investments in rangeland rehabilitation. This will not make the investment attractive for private sector funding.  

### 64. The project as conceptualized and presented in the funding proposal and its relevant annexes does not entail the risk of crowding-out private and other public investments. Success will instead produce a convincing argument that a conducive environment exists and should catalyse further private sector investments.  

## 1.6.2. Co-financing/leveraging  

### 65. According to the information included in the funding proposal, with a contribution of USD 29.99 million from GCF, the project will have a grant to co-financing ratio of 1:0.7. The co-financing will total USD 20 million, of which: 77 per cent will be from RKDF; 18 per cent from beneficiaries; and about 2 per cent each from SAEPF, MAFIM and ARIS. FAO will also contribute USD 400,000 to support policy dialogue around evidence-based NRM governance. Given this information, it can be concluded that the project will leverage an adequate level of co-financing, as determined by project needs from the other partners.  

### 66. As stated in paragraph 64 above, the success of this project will produce a conducive environment in Kyrgyzstan that will catalyse further investment, particularly from the private sector, to finance similar investments in the future.

# GCF/B.24/02/Add.02  
## Page 137  

### Analysis  
The following metrics were provided for the expected financial performance of the project for a 20-year analysis:  
- (a) Expected internal rate of return = 71.3 per cent; and  
- (b) Estimated net present value (E-NPV) = USD 353.7 million.  

### Sensitivity Analysis  
68. Robust sensitivity analyses covering parameters such as increased cost of the project due to climate change impacts, and decrease of benefits due to climate change impacts, were carried out. The result of the analysis showed that variation of these parameters between 20 and 30 per cent will still yield acceptable results with the highest decrease in the estimated net present value from the base case of about 52 per cent when the benefits due to climate change impacts decrease by about 30 per cent. Even for this scenario, the estimated net present value will be USD 170.7 million while the expected internal rate of return will be about 41.2 per cent.  

### Project Design  
69. From the review of the plans reported in the funding proposal and its annexes, it can be concluded that the project incorporated best practices and available technologies in its design. Multiple options for each type of intervention proposed were assessed before finalization of the design, which has now been presented for implementation.  

### iTAP Conclusion  
70. Based on the discussions presented above, the iTAP concludes that the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed CS-FOR project are “high”.  

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
71. The iTAP therefore recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# CS-FOR  
## Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan 

### 1. A Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan (GAP) was prepared to implement the gender strategy and to ensure inclusion of disadvantaged groups (poor families, single-headed households and youth).

The table below presents specific actions in each project component with indicative budget as envisaged at the proposal stage. Minimum 30 percent was set as a target in accordance with the FAO’s operational work and budget at the country and regional levels is allocated to women-specific targeted interventions.

### 2. Female-headed households, unemployed, youth, disabled groups will be prioritized as a vulnerable group to be targeted in the project activities. All trainings will have post-training evaluation questionnaires to track the usefulness of the trainings. Gender assessment and monitoring will help to track how the skills are being adopted that were acquired in trainings, how knowledge and equipment was used by women to improve their businesses.

### 3. The budget for the Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan (GAP) was calculated according to the underlying principles:

#### a. For activities related to capacity development / training / awareness campaigns: 30% of the budget is considered gender sensitive. This is related to the project target of women inclusion in such kind of activities.

#### b. For activities involving policy dialogue / regulatory framework: 50% of the budget is considered gender sensitive. This is due to the estimates on beneficiaries of project interventions.

#### c. For activities involving investment in rangeland and forestry (comp 2 – excluding training): 50% of the budget is considered gender sensitive. This is due to the estimates on beneficiaries of project interventions.

#### d. For activities related to investment in climate sensitive value chains (comp 3 – excluding training): 10% of the budget is considered gender sensitive, considering the estimated potential outreach of the project.

### 4. On the basis of the final GAP, the Gender and Social Development Specialist each year prepares an annual work plan and budget of GAP for submission to the Project Director.

### 5. The GAP is in line with the overall project implementation plan and timeline, thus all activities are incorporated into the relevant components of the project.

### 6. Capacity development activities will be carried out not only by providing trainings for beneficiaries (both women and men) but also ensuring access of women and female headed households to decision-making processes, such as securing quotas (at least 30%) for memberships in CLMGs, close collaboration with and promotion of gender sensitization among the local government and core district-level institutions (PUUs, WUAs) responsible for the management of access to natural resources (water, pastures, land and other resources). 

The project will ensure a specific approach for women when partnering with financial institutions and working on financial education of women entrepreneurs, supporting the latter one VCD. Through these activities it is anticipated to work on sustainability of the project interventions.

# CS-FOR  
## Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### 10  

#### 6. GAP planning and implementation  
At the onset of the project implementation, the EG, under the overall responsibility of PIU Project Manager, with the technical support of the Gender and Social Development Specialist, will organize a workshop to validate the proposed GAP and sensitize key stakeholders. This would be followed by briefings and capacity development activities on GAP for the project team, implementation partners and other relevant stakeholders (refresher training will be organized later on as and when appropriate). A gender focal point will be nominated for each implementing partner, who will coordinate all issues related to gender mainstreaming and social inclusion. Gender-responsive mobilization and communication channels will be developed to reach both women and men, including vulnerable groups (such as female-headed households, low income families, unemployed). On the basis of the final GAP, the Gender and Social Development Specialist each year prepares an annual work plan and budget of GAP for submission to the Project Director.  

#### 7. Project Manager will be held accountable for gender mainstreaming and social inclusion of the project, technically supported by the Gender and Social development specialist. This specialist will coordinate all work on gender mainstreaming in collaboration with the gender focal points of partner institutions and other stakeholders, the FAO Lead Technical Officer (LTO) and other members of the project team.  

#### 8. Implementation of GAP will be supported by relevant FAO gender equality tools, such as FAO Gender mainstreaming and human rights-based approach: guidelines for technical officers 2 and the VGGT technical guide: governing land for women and men 3. In addition, to promote and share its best practices FAO developed policies, guidelines and valuable tools in gender-mainstreaming:  

- In 2012, FAO endorsed a Policy on Gender Equality, the goal of which is to achieve equality between women and men in sustainable agricultural production and rural development, with the aim of eliminating hunger and poverty4.  
- Regional Gender Equality Strategy for 2019–2022 represents a common vision of what FAO intends to achieve over the next four years in promoting gender equality and women’s empowerment through its interventions in the region, and how5  
- Gender mainstreaming and human rights-based approach. Guidelines for technical officers6.  
- Agri-Gender Statistics Toolkit7  
- Environment and Social Management Guidelines8.  
- Developing gender-sensitive value chains. A guiding framework9  
- FAO-Adapt10  
- Climate-smart agriculture11
